Section Three · From beginner to professional

Programming languages

Five languages cover most of what you'll need, in learning order: HTML to build the page structure, CSS to style it, JavaScript to make it interactive, Python for general programming and AI, and C++ for systems and high performance. Each guide explains the idea simply before the code, then applies it in a complete example.

In simple wordsA language is how you write instructions for a computer. Start with HTML for structure, CSS for looks, JavaScript for interaction, then Python for general work and C++ for performance.
5 languages Explanations + copyable examples A learning plan for every language

HTML

A markup language that defines a page's structure and the meaning of its content. It is not a programming language, but it is the foundation of every website.

Level: Beginner · Duration: 2–3 weeks
In simple wordsHTML is the structure of a page: headings, paragraphs, images, and links. Not a programming language in the full sense — it describes content and meaning.

What is HTML?

HTML stands for HyperText Markup Language. The page is described with elements such as a title, paragraph, image, link, and form. The browser reads this description, builds a DOM tree, and then displays it. HTML alone produces content without formatting or interactivity; CSS is for form and JavaScript is for behavior.

Analogy:

HTML is the structure of the house (walls and doors), CSS is the finishing and colors, and JavaScript is the electricity and movement. It is not possible to finish a house without a structure.

The basic structure of any page

html
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="ar" dir="rtl">
<head>
  <meta charset="UTF-8">
  <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1">
  <title>Page title</title>
  <link rel="stylesheet" href="style.css">
</head>
<body>
  <h1>Hello, world</h1>
  <p>My first page.</p>
  <script src="app.js" defer></script>
</body>
</html>

The DOCTYPE tells the browser that the page is HTML5. lang and dir are important for Arabic language and screen readers. viewport is necessary for correct display on the phone. charset="UTF-8" Prevents Arabic letters from appearing distorted.

Semantic elements and tags

An element often consists of an opening tag, content, and closing tag: <p>text</p>. Some elements, such as <img> and <br>, are empty and do not need to be closed. Semantic elements tell the browser and search engines the meaning of the content:

ItemMeaningUse
<header>HeaderLogo, title, main navigation.
<nav>NavigationSection links and menus.
<main>Main contentOnce per page.
<section>SectionA titled content collection.
<article>Independent articleNews or post that can be shared individually.
<aside>Side contentAdvertisements or supporting information.
<footer>FooterRights, links, contact information.
html
<body>
  <header>
    <h1>My blog</h1>
    <nav>
      <a href="#en-posts">Articles</a>
      <a href="#en-about">About me</a>
    </nav>
  </header>

  <main>
    <article>
      <h2>First article</h2>
      <p>The text of the article is here.</p>
    </article>
  </main>

  <footer><p>© 2026</p></footer>
</body>

Use <div> and <span> only when there is no suitable semantic element. div is a block container, and span is an inline container.

Headings and text

Headings from h1 to h6, preferably one h1 heading per page and then a logical progression. Do not choose a heading by its size, but by its importance, then style it with CSS.

html
<h1>Headline</h1>
<h2>Subtitle</h2>
<p>Normal paragraph with <strong>Important text</strong> <em>tighten</em>.</p>
<blockquote>Quote from source.</blockquote>
<pre><code>console.log("code");</code></pre>
<hr>
  • <strong> conveys semantic importance, while <b> is only visual boldness.
  • <em> conveys semantic emphasis, while <i> is only visual italics.
  • <br> Breaks a line within text; do not use it to add spacing between elements.

Links, images and media

html
<!-- Internal link-->
<a href="#en-section-1">Go to section</a>

<!-- External link opens in a new tab -->
<a href="https://example.com" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">
  Visit the website
</a>

<!--Image with alt text-->
<img src="images/team.jpg" alt="Work team in meeting" width="600" >

<!--Video-->
<video controls poster="cover.jpg">
  <source src="intro.mp4" type="video/mp4">
  Your browser does not support video.
</video>

The alternative text alt is not optional: it appears when an image fails to load, is read by a screen reader, and helps search engines. Use loading="lazy" for images at the bottom of the page to improve performance.

Lists and tables

html
<ul>
  <li>Unordered item</li>
  <li>Other item</li>
</ul>

<ol>
  <li>Step 1</li>
  <li>Step 2</li>
</ol>

<table>
  <caption>Test results</caption>
  <thead>
    <tr><th scope="col">Name</th><th scope="col">Class</th></tr>
  </thead>
  <tbody>
    <tr><td>Ali</td> <td>95</td></tr>
  </tbody>
</table>

Do not use tables for page layout; this was the style of the 90s. The table is for tabular data only. Use scope to link cells to titles for screen readers.

Forms

The form is a data entry portal. Each field must be linked to <label> via for and id, which is necessary for accessibility and click-through of the text.

html
<form action="/subscribe" method="post">
  <label for="en-email">Email</label>
  <input id="en-email" name="email" type="email" required
         placeholder="name@example.com" autocomplete="email">

  <label for="en-password">Password</label>
  <input id="en-password" name="password" type="password"
         minlength="8" required>

  <label for="en-role">Role</label>
  <select id="en-role" name="role">
    <option value="dev">Developer</option>
    <option value="designer">Designer</option>
  </select>

  <label>
    <input type="checkbox" name="terms" required>
    I agree to the terms
  </label>

  <button type="submit">Submit</button>
</form>
Validation in the interface is not enough:

The required attribute and type="email" improve the experience but can easily be bypassed. True verification and security should always happen on the server.

Accessibility

Accessibility is not a luxury: it makes your site usable for everyone, and improves SEO. Start with the basics:

  • Alt text for each image, and logical, hierarchical titles.
  • Sufficient color contrast between text and background.
  • Do not rely on color alone to convey meaning.
  • label for each field, and aria-label for icon-only buttons.
  • Keyboard navigation and a clear focus indicator.
html
<button aria-label="Close window">✕</button>
<img src="chart.png" alt="First quarter sales rise 20%">
<html lang="ar" dir="rtl">

Best practices

✔ Do

Semantic structure, meaningful headings, comments only when needed, and clear lowercase filenames.

✘ Avoid

Deprecated tags such as <font> and <center>, inline formatting in HTML, and duplicate id.

  • Check your page via W3C Validator for syntax errors.
  • Use id once and class for repeated styles.
  • Make HTML clean and readable; you will come back to it often.
  • Do not place important texts inside images.

Two-week learning plan

1

Days 1–4: Structure and tags

Write a full bio page with tags without any CSS.

2

Days 5–8: Forms and tables

Create a registration form and table with proper headings and linking.

3

Days 9–12: Accessibility

Review your page with a screen reader or Lighthouse tool and improve the result.

4

Days 13–14: Project

Build a full landing page for a mock product, then style it with CSS.

CSS

A stylesheet language that defines a page's appearance: colors, fonts, spacing, layout, and responsiveness. Without it, HTML remains plain text.

Level: Beginner · Duration: 3–4 weeks
In simple wordsCSS is the look of a page: colours, spacing, fonts, and layout. You pick an element and change its appearance.

What is CSS?

CSS stands for Cascading Style Sheets. It works with a simple rule: a selector (what to target) plus declarations (property: value). The browser applies rules sequentially and resolves conflicts according to priority and inheritance.

css
/* delimiter + declarations */
.card {
  background: #0f1520;
  color: #eef2f9;
  padding: 20px;
  border-radius: 12px;
}

Ways to add CSS

  • External: <link rel="stylesheet" href="style.css"> — Always the best.
  • Internal: <style> in the head — for small pages or experiments.
  • Inline: style="..." — Avoid as it is difficult to maintain and mixes content with appearance.

Selectors

SelectorExampleMeaning
ElementpAll paragraphs.
class.cardAll items with class="card".
id#headerOnly one item with this id.
Descendant.card pAll p inside card.
Direct child.card > pp direct only.
Pseudo-classa:hoverWhen the cursor passes.
Attributeinput[type="email"]Specific email field.
Grouph1, h2, h3Apply the same rule to all three.

Priority

When two rules conflict, the more specific one wins: inline, then id, then class/attribute/pseudo-class, then element. If the priority is equal, the last one in source order wins. Avoid !important unless absolutely necessary.

css
p { color: gray; }              /* 0,0,1 */
.note { color: blue; }          /* 0,1,0 */
.note.warning { color: orange; }/* 0,2,0 */
#main .note { color: red; }     /* 1,1,0 */

Box Model

Each CSS element is a box made up of four layers from inside to outside: content, then padding, then border, then margin. Understanding this model solves 80% of layout problems.

css
*, *::before, *::after {
  box-sizing: border-box;   /* width includes padding + border */
}

.box {
  width: 300px;
  padding: 20px;
  border: 2px solid #2e3c53;
  margin: 16px auto;
}
Why border-box?

Without it, an element with a width of 300px, a padding of 20px, and a border of 2px has an actual width of 344px and causes horizontal scrolling. With border-box 300px remains fixed.

Flexbox — layout in one dimension

Flexbox is excellent for arranging items in a row or column, spacing, and alignment. Set display: flex on the parent and then control the children.

css
.toolbar {
  display:flex;
  align-items: center;        /* vertical alignment */
  justify-content: space-between; /* Horizontal distribution */
  gap: 12px;
  flex-wrap: wrap;            /* Drops to a new line when narrow */
}

.toolbar .grow {
  flex: 1;                    /* Takes the remaining space */
}
PropertyCommon valuesEffect
flex-directionrow · columnDirection of the major axis.
justify-contentflex-start · center · space-betweenDistribution on the main axis.
align-itemsstretch · center · flex-startAlignment on the cross axis.
gap8px · 1remDistance between children.
flex1 · 0 0 autoThe ability of an element to grow and shrink.

Grid — layout in two dimensions

CSS Grid distributes elements into both rows and columns. It is the first choice for full page or card grid layout.

css
.cards {
  display: grid;
  grid-template-columns: repeat(auto-fit, minmax(240px, 1fr));
  gap: 16px;
}

.layout {
  display: grid;
  grid-template-columns: 260px minmax(0, 1fr);
  gap: 30px;
  align-items: start;
}

repeat(auto-fit, minmax(240px, 1fr)) One line builds a fully responsive grid: each column is at least 240px, and the number of columns changes automatically depending on the width. This style eliminates the need for many media queries.

Responsive design

Design for mobile first, then expand. Use relative units such as rem, %, and clamp(), images with a maximum of max-width: 100%, and then media queries at the actual content breakpoints.

css
html { font-size: 16px; }

h1 { font-size: clamp(1.75rem, 4vw, 3rem); }

img { max-width: 100%; height: auto; }

@media (max-width: 880px) {
  .layout { grid-template-columns: 1fr; }
  .sidebar { display: none; }
}

Don't forget the viewport tag in your HTML. Without it, responsive design won't work on the phone at all. Always test at 320px, 768px, and 1440px widths.

Variables, states, and animation

CSS variables are defined in :root and used as var(). Changing them in one place changes the entire design, and they are the basis of night mode and themes.

css
:root {
  --bg: #080b11;
  --text: #eef2f9;
  --accent: #7c8cf8;
  --radius: 16px;
}

.card {
  background: var(--bg);
  color: var(--text);
  border-radius: var(--radius);
}

.card:hover {
  border-color: var(--accent);
  transform: translateY(-3px);
}

.card {
  transition: transform .18s ease, border-color .18s ease;
}
css
@keyframes fadeUp {
  from { opacity: 0; transform: translateY(10px); }
  to   { opacity: 1; transform: none; }
}

.panel { animation: fadeUp .3s ease both; }

@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce) {
  * { animation: none !important; transition: none !important; }
}

Always respect the prefers-reduced-motion setting; some users experience dizziness from motion.

Best practices

✔ Do

Clear class names, color variables, Flex/Grid layout, logical property ordering, and testing on different sizes.

✘ Avoid

!important repeated, over-specific selectors, hard-coded colors, and fixed text heights.

  • Use gap to replace margins between elements in Flex/Grid.
  • Don't place text over images without sufficient opacity and contrast.
  • Check contrast with tools like WebAIM Contrast Checker.
  • Organize CSS into sections with comments, or use Tailwind/SCSS on larger projects.

3-week learning plan

1

Week 1: The basics

Selectors, colors, texts, Box Model. Layout a simple HTML page entirely.

2

Week 2: Layout

Flexbox then Grid. Rebuild the layout of a popular website as an exercise.

3

Week 3: Professional polish

Responsiveness, variables, animation, and accessibility. Build a full landing page and publish it.

JavaScript

The only language that runs natively in the browser and also on the server via Node.js. It is the language of events, interaction, and modern applications.

Level: Beginner · Duration: 4–6 weeks
In simple wordsJavaScript is the interaction: it reacts to clicks, changes the page, and talks to the server.

Why JavaScript?

Every interactive web page uses JavaScript: drop-down menus, form validation, refresh without reloading, and animation. And with Node.js now being used on the server as well, you can build complete applications in one language. Frameworks like React, Vue, and Next.js are built on it, and it is the most in-demand language in the web market.

In the browser

It controls the page via the DOM and responds to user events.

On the server

Node.js allows building APIs and real-time services.

Huge projects

npm is the largest package system in the world.

Important information:

JavaScript is not Java. Similar in name only; they are two completely different languages in design and usage.

Environment and operation

Fastest way to try: Open the developer tools in your browser (F12) and type in the Console tab. To run a file on your device you need Node.js from nodejs.org, then:

bash
node --version
node app.js
npm init -y
npm install axios

To link JavaScript to an HTML page, place the <script> tag before the closing </body>, with defer so the page loads first:

html
<script src="app.js" defer></script>

Variables and types

Use const by default, let when you need to reassign, and avoid var as its range may cause unexpected errors. JavaScript has dynamic types like Python.

javascript
const name = "Ahmed";
let age = 21;
const isStudent = true;
const skills = ["JS", "CSS"];
const user = { id: 1, name: "Sara" };

console. log(typeof age);          // "number"
console.log(`${name} is ${age}`);

// The difference between == and ===
console.log(5 == "5");            // true (type conversion)
console.log(5 === "5");           // false (different type)

Always use === and !== to avoid confusing implicit conversion. The values null, undefined, 0, "", and NaN are all Falsy in the logical context.

Functions and scope

A regular function is declared with function, and an arrow function (=>) is shorter and does not have its own this. Scope determines where the variable can be accessed: let and const are limited to block { }.

javascript
function add(a, b) {
  return a + b;
}

const multiply = (a, b) => a * b;

const greet = (name = "visitor") => {
  const message = `Hello ${name}`;
  return message;
};

console.log(add(2, 3));
console.log(multiply(4, 5));
console.log(greet());

Closure is the ability of a function to remember variables from the scope where it was created even after it has finished. This principle is the basis of many patterns in JavaScript:

javascript
function counter() {
  let count = 0;
  return () => ++count;
}

const next = counter();
console.log(next());   // 1
console.log(next());   // 2

Arrays and objects

The three most important functions in daily work: map for transformation, filter for selection, and reduce for aggregation. They all return a new value and do not modify the original.

javascript
const nums = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6];

const doubled = nums.map(n => n * 2);
const evens = nums.filter(n => n % 2 === 0);
const total = nums.reduce((sum, n) => sum + n, 0);

console.log(doubled);   // [2, 4, 6, 8, 10, 12]
console.log(evens);     // [2, 4, 6]
console.log(total);     // 21

Destructuring and spreading make object handling cleaner:

javascript
const user = { name: "Ali", age: 25, city: "Cairo" };
const { name, age } = user;

const updated = { ...user, age: 26 };
const first = [1, 2];
const all = [...first, 3, 4];

console.log(name, age);
console.log(updated);
console.log(all);

Working with the page (DOM)

DOM is the tree representation of a page. JavaScript can select elements, change their content and attributes, and respond to user events. Select elements with querySelector, and listen for events with addEventListener.

html
<input id="en-task" placeholder="New task">
<button id="en-add">Add</button>
<ul id="en-list"></ul>
javascript
const input = document.querySelector("#task");
const button = document.querySelector("#add");
const list = document.querySelector("#list");

button.addEventListener("click", () => {
  const text = input.value.trim();
  if (!text) return;

  const li = document.createElement("li");
  li.textContent = text;
  li.addEventListener("click", () => li.remove());
  list.appendChild(li);
  input.value = "";
});

This example brings it all together: reading a value, validating it, creating an element, adding an event listener, and modifying the DOM. Try it on a blank page and you will understand the full workflow.

Async: Promises and async/await

JavaScript is single-threaded, so it doesn't wait for slow operations like the network, but rather registers a function that executes when it completes. fetch returns a Promise, and async/await makes it read like normal sequential code.

javascript
async function getUsers() {
  try {
    const res = await fetch("https://jsonplaceholder.typicode.com/users");
    if (!res.ok) throw new Error(`HTTP ${res.status}`);

    const users = await res.json();
    return users.map(u => u.name);
  } catch (err) {
    console.error("Fetch failed:", err.message);
    return [];
  }
}

getUsers().then(names => console.log(names));
Common mistake:

Forgetting await before res.json(), the variable holds a Promise instead of the data. Any function that returns a Promise must be waited for or processed with .then().

Modules

Split the code into files. Use export to export what you want to share and import to use it. In the browser, add type="module" to the script tag.

javascript
// math.js
export const add = (a, b) => a + b;
export default function multiply(a, b) {
  return a * b;
}

// app.js
import multiply, { add } from "./math.js";
console.log(add(2, 3), multiply(2, 3));

Best practices and common mistakes

✔ Do

Use const, clear names, lowercase function names, async/await with try/catch, and check the inputs.

✘ Avoid

Global variables, var, innerHTML with user data (XSS risk), modifying the DOM inside a loop.

  • this: In arrow functions this inherits from the surrounding scope, not from the element that called the function.
  • Arrays: forEach does not return a value; use map if you want a new result.
  • Comparison: NaN === NaN gives false, use Number.isNaN().
  • Security: Do not put secrets or API keys in the interface code, they are visible to everyone.

30-day learning plan

1

Days 1–7: The basics

Variables, conditions, loops, functions. Apply each lesson in your browser's console.

2

Days 8–14: Arrays and objects

map/filter/reduce, parsing, and handling JSON.

3

Days 15–21: DOM and events

Build a complete to-do list with adding, deleting, and saving to localStorage.

4

Days 22–30: API and project

Fetch data from a public API and display it with loading and error states, then publish the project.

Python

A high-level, readable, and powerful language used in almost every field: automation, web, data analysis, artificial intelligence, and cybersecurity.

Level: Beginner · Duration: 4–6 weeks
In simple wordsPython reads almost like English: use it for scripts, data, AI, and servers with very little ceremony.

Why Python?

Python is designed to be clear and close to English, so you spend less time on syntax and stay focused on solving the problem. It is the leading language in artificial intelligence and data analysis, one of the most widely used in automation and penetration testing (many Kali tools are written in it), and is used in the web via Django, Flask, and FastAPI.

Easy to learn

Clean syntax without complicated punctuation, suitable as first language.

Huge libraries

Over 500K packages on PyPI covering every domain.

Broad labor market

In demand in data, AI, DevOps, and security.

Note about performance:

Python is not the fastest because it is interpreted and not pre-compiled. But in most applications, the bottleneck is in the database or network, not in the language, and when needed, critical parts can be accelerated with libraries written in C.

Installation and setup

Download Python from python.org (choose version 3.12 or later), and during installation on Windows make sure the Add Python to PATH option is enabled. Then check the installation in the terminal.

bash
python --version        # Windows
python3 --version       # Linux / macOS
pip --version

Each project has an independent virtual environment so that packages do not conflict between projects:

bash
python -m venv .venv
# Windows
.venv\Scripts\activate
# Linux / macOS
source .venv/bin/activate

pip install requests
pip freeze > requirements.txt

Suggested text editor: VS Code with official Python plugin. Run the file with the command python main.py, or use the interactive terminal python to quickly try a single line.

Basics: variables and types

Python is dynamically typed: you do not need to declare a variable's type; it is inferred automatically. Names are case sensitive and are usually written in snake_case style.

python
name = "Ahmed"        # str
age = 21               # int
height = 1.78          # float
is_student = True      # bool
skills = ["Python", "SQL"]   # list
profile = {"city": "Cairo"}  # dict
nothing = None         # NoneType

print(type(age))              # <class 'int'>
print(f"{name} is {age}")     # f-string
print(name.upper(), len(name))

Conditions and loops

Indentation is not just formatting in Python; it is part of the language's syntax. Use 4 spaces, and do not confuse spaces with Tab.

python
score = 82

if score >= 90:
    grade = "A"
elif score >= 80:
    grade = "B"
else:
    grade = "C"

for i in range(1, 4):
    print(i)            # 1 2 3

count = 3
while count > 0:
    print(count)
    count -= 1

Note that range(1, 4) stops before 4. This is intentional and frequent behavior in Python: the end is not included.

Data structures

Choosing the right structure solves half the problem. The list is ordered and editable, the tuple is fixed, the dictionary stores key/value pairs, and the set prevents duplication.

StructureSyntaxWhen do you use it?
list[1, 2, 3]An ordered, mutable collection (add/delete).
tuple(1, 2, 3)Static data such as coordinates or settings.
dict{"name": "Ali"}Fast key search and representation of JSON objects.
set{1, 2, 3}Remove duplicates and test membership quickly.
python
nums = [4, 8, 15, 16, 23, 42]

squares = [n * n for n in nums if n % 2 == 0]
print(squares)                 # [16, 64, 256, 1764]

total = sum(nums)
largest = max(nums)
unique = set([1, 2, 2, 3])     # {1, 2, 3}

user = {"name": "Sara", "age": 24}
print(user.get("email", "not available"))

for key, value in user.items():
    print(key, value)

List Comprehension is Python's preferred method for constructing a list from another list. You read it from the right: “For every n in nums, if even, calculate n*n.”

Functions and modules

The function encapsulates reusable logic. Use def, specify default values when needed, and return an explicit value with return.

python
def greet(name, greeting="Hello"):
    return f"{greeting} {name}!"

print(greet("Ali"))
print(greet("Sara", "Hi"))

def average(*numbers):
    return sum(numbers) / len(numbers) if numbers else 0

print(average(10, 20, 30))    # 20.0

*args groups positional arguments into a tuple, and **kwargs groups named arguments into a dict. Use them when you don't know the number of arguments in advance.

python
# math_tools.py
def add(a, b):
    return a + b

# main.py
import math_tools
from math_tools import add

print(math_tools.add(2, 3))
print(add(4, 5))

Object-Oriented Programming (OOP)

The object combines data (properties) and behavior (methods) into a single unit. The class is the template, and the object is a copy of it. Basic concepts: encapsulation, inheritance, and polymorphism.

python
class Account:
    def __init__(self, owner, balance=0):
        self.owner = owner
        self.balance = balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        if amount <= 0:
            raise ValueError("The amount must be positive")
        self.balance += amount

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        if amount > self.balance:
            raise ValueError("Insufficient balance")
        self.balance -= amount

    def __str__(self):
        return f"{self.owner}: {self.balance}"

acc = Account("Ahmed", 100)
acc.deposit(50)
acc.withdraw(30)
print(acc)          # Ahmed: 120

__init__ is the constructor, and self refers to the current object. Functions that begin and end with a double underscore are called dunder methods and control the object's behavior with language operations such as printing and comparing.

Files and exceptions

Use with to open files; it ensures that the file is closed even if an error occurs. Don't swallow mistakes silently: catch the ones you understand, handle them, and let the rest surface.

python
from pathlib import Path

path = Path("notes.txt")
path.write_text("First line\n", encoding="utf-8")
with path.open("a", encoding="utf-8") as f:
    f.write("Newline\n")

try:
    number = int(input("Enter number: "))
    print(10 / number)
exceptValueError:
    print("This is not a valid number")
except ZeroDivisionError:
    print("Cannot be divided by zero")
finally:
    print("Execution completed")

To read JSON, which is the most popular form of data exchange between services:

python
import json

data = {"name": "Ali", "skills": ["Python", "SQL"]}
text = json.dumps(data, ensure_ascii=False, indent=2)
parsed = json.loads(text)
print(parsed["skills"][0])

Libraries you should know

LibraryDomainWhat is it for?
requestsnetworksSend HTTP requests easily.
pandasDataSpreadsheets, analysis, and data cleaning.
numpyMathematicsFast array operations.
flask / fastapiWebBuilding APIs and web services.
djangoWebFull framework for large sites.
pytestTestingWriting and running tests.
beautifulsoup4ScrapingExtract data from HTML.
scikit-learnArtificial intelligenceReady-made machine learning models.

Best practices and common mistakes

✔ Do

Clear names, small functions, comments that explain “why” not “what”, use venv for every project, and write tests.

✘ Avoid

Global variables, except:, untyped code, modifying a list while iterating over it, and repeating code instead of extracting a function.

  • Error: Forgetting that lists are passed by reference — modifying them inside a function affects the original.
  • Error: Using == to compare with None; the correct form is is None.
  • Error: Relying on dict ordering in older versions (guaranteed from 3.7+).
  • Error: Installing packages globally instead of in a virtual environment.

30-day learning plan

1

Days 1–7: The basics

Variables, conditions, loops, functions. Solve 3 problems a day on a platform like HackerRank.

2

Days 8–14: Data structures

Lists, dictionaries, and sets, then solve text and numerical problems.

3

Days 15–21: Files and OOP

Build a notes app that saves in JSON, then rewrite it using a class.

4

Days 22–30: Real project

A small API or command-line tool with FastAPI, with a README and tests on GitHub.

C++

A high-performance compiled language that combines procedural and object-oriented programming. It is used in systems, games, browsers, and engines that cannot tolerate slowdowns.

Level: Intermediate · Duration: 8–12 weeks
In simple wordsC++ is fast and powerful: you control memory and performance, which is why systems, games, and heavy software use it.

Why C++?

C++ gives you direct control over memory and performance and compiles to very fast machine code. That is why it is used in game engines (Unreal), browsers (Chrome), operating systems, databases, and low-latency financial applications. It is a relatively difficult language because you are responsible for managing the resources yourself.

Should you start with it?

If your goal is web or data, start with Python or JavaScript. Choose C++ when you need high performance, work close to hardware, or study computer science in depth.

Environment and compilation

You need a compiler and an editor. On Windows use Visual Studio with the “Desktop development with C++” workload, or MinGW/g++. On Linux install build-essential. The files are written with the extension .cpp and compiled into an executable program.

bash
g++ --version
g++ main.cpp -o app -std=c++17 -Wall -Wextra
./app

-std=c++17 specifies the standard version, and -Wall -Wextra activates important warnings. For large projects use CMake for build management instead of long command lines.

cpp
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    std::cout << "Hello, World!\n";
    return 0;
}

#include imports a library, std::cout prints to the screen, and return 0 means the program was successful. Every C++ program starts at main.

Variables and types

C++ is statically typed: the type of each variable must be declared, and the compiler prevents unsafe conversions. This detects errors early and speeds up execution.

cpp
#include <iostream>
#include <string>

int main() {
    int age = 21;
    double height = 1.78;
    char grade = 'A';
    bool isStudent = true;
    std::string name = "Ahmed";

    const double PI = 3.14159;   // a constant that never changes

    std::cout << name << " " << age << '\n';
    std::cout << "Height: " << height << '\n';
    return 0;
}

Use const for every value that does not change, and auto when the type is clear from the context to reduce redundancy.

Conditions and loops

cpp
int score = 82;

if (score >= 90) {
    std::cout << "A\n";
} else if (score >= 80) {
    std::cout << "B\n";
} else {
    std::cout << "C\n";
}

for (int i = 1; i <= 3; ++i) {
    std::cout << i << '\n';
}

int count = 3;
while (count > 0) {
    std::cout << count-- << '\n';
}

++i increments and returns the new value, while i++ returns the old value and then increments. The difference is important in complex expressions, and in loops ++i is preferred.

Functions and references

Passing variables in C++ can be by value (a copy), by reference & (modifies the original), or with a pointer *. For large objects use const & to avoid copying while preventing modification.

cpp
#include <iostream>

void addFive(int x) { x += 5; } // Copy: The original does not change

void addFiveRef(int& x) { x += 5; } // Reference: The parent changes

int sum(const int& a, const int& b) { // read without copying
    return a + b;
}

int main() {
    int n = 10;
    addFive(n);
    std::cout << n << '\n';   // 10
    addFiveRef(n);
    std::cout << n << '\n';   // 15
    std::cout << sum(2, 3) << '\n';
}

Arrays, vector and string

The traditional array has a fixed size. std::vector from the standard library expands automatically and is the default in most cases. std::string manages strings safely.

cpp
#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
#include <string>

int main() {
    std::vector<int> nums = {4, 8, 15, 16};

    nums.push_back(23);
    for (int n : nums) {
        std::cout << n << ' ';
    }

    std::cout << "\nSize: " << nums.size() << '\n';
    std::cout << "First: " << nums.front() << '\n';

    std::string name = "Ahmed";
    name += " Rashid";
    std::cout << name << '\n';
}
Array bounds:

C++ does not automatically check bounds. Accessing nums[100] in a 5-element vector is undefined behavior and the program may crash. Use .at() if you need a check, or nums.size() before accessing.

Pointers and memory

A pointer is a variable that holds the address of another variable in memory. &x means “the address of x”, and *p means “the value at that address”. Pointers are powerful, but they are the primary source of bugs and crashes.

cpp
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    int value = 42;
    int*ptr = &value;

    std::cout << value << '\n';   // 42
    std::cout << ptr << '\n';     //Memory address
    std::cout << *ptr << '\n';    // 42

    *ptr = 100;
    std::cout << value << '\n';   // 100
}

Dynamic memory is reserved with new and freed with delete. Forgetting to delete causes a memory leak, and deleting twice causes a crash. So smart pointers are used instead:

cpp
#include <memory>
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    auto p = std::make_unique<int>(42);
    std::cout << *p << '\n';
    // Automatically released when p goes out of range (RAII)
}

Object-oriented programming

The C++ class defines properties and methods, with fine-grained access control via public, private, and protected. The constructor initializes the object, and the destructor ~Class cleans up the resources.

cpp
#include <iostream>
#include <string>
#include <utility>

class Account {
private:
    std::string owner;
    double balance;

public:
    Account(std::string name, double initial = 0)
        : owner(std::move(name)), balance(initial) {}

    void deposit(double amount) {
        if (amount <= 0) return;
        balance += amount;
    }

    void withdraw(double amount) {
        if (amount > balance) return;
        balance -= amount;
    }

    void print() const {
        std::cout << owner << ": " << balance << '\n';
    }
};

int main() {
    Account acc("Ahmed", 100);
    acc.deposit(50);
    acc.withdraw(30);
    acc.print();       // Ahmed: 120
}

Inheritance allows one class to be built on top of another, and virtual functions (virtual) enable polymorphism: the same call implements the appropriate behavior for the actual object.

cpp
class Shape {
public:
    virtual double area() const = 0;   // pure virtual function
    virtual ~Shape() = default;
};

class Circle : public Shape {
    double r;
public:
    explicit Circle(double radius) : r(radius) {}
    double area() const override { return 3.14159 * r * r; }
};

class Square : public Shape {
    double side;
public:
    explicit Square(double s) : side(s) {}
    double area() const override { return side * side; }
};

The C++ Standard Library (STL)

The STL provides highly efficient containers and algorithms. Don't rewrite what already exists:

ComponentUse
vectorDynamic array — most commonly used.
arrayFixed-size array, safer.
map / unordered_mapkey/value map; unordered is often faster.
setUnique values in order.
queue / stackFIFO queue and LIFO stack.
sort / find / countReady-made algorithms in <algorithm>.
cpp
#include <iostream>
#include <vector>
#include <algorithm>
#include <map>
#include <string>

int main() {
    std::vector<int> nums = {5, 2, 9, 1};
    std::sort(nums.begin(), nums.end());

    for (int n : nums) std::cout << n << ' ';
    std::cout << '\n';

    std::map<std::string, int> ages;
    ages["Ali"] = 25;
    ages["Sara"] = 24;

    for (const auto& [name, age] : ages) {
        std::cout << name << ": " << age << '\n';
    }
}

Mistakes and best practices

✔ Do

Use std::vector and std::string, pass const &, use smart pointers, enable warnings.

✘ Avoid

Raw arrays and bare pointers, manual new/delete, using namespace std; in large files, forgetting break in switch.

  • Memory leak: Every new needs a delete — and it is best to avoid them entirely.
  • Out-of-bounds access: undefined behavior; use .at() during development.
  • Uninitialized variables: may hold arbitrary values; always initialize them.
  • Copying large objects: use references to avoid hidden slowdowns.

12-week learning plan

1

Weeks 1–3: Basics

Types, conditions, loops, functions. Solve simple logical and mathematical exercises.

2

Weeks 4–6: Memory and structures

Arrays, vector, string, pointers, and references. Draw the memory on paper.

3

Weeks 7–9: OOP and STL

Classes, inheritance, polymorphism, then containers and algorithms.

4

Weeks 10–12: Project

A small terminal app or game library management system, with CMake, tests, and a README.

Training projects for each language

Videos teach you syntax, but projects teach you programming. Start with a small project you can finish in two days, then expand it gradually. This table has a project for every level in every language.

LanguageBeginner projectIntermediate projectAdvanced project
PythonCalculator + task manager in the terminalA script that collects data from an API and saves it in SQLiteFull API with FastAPI, authentication, and tests
JavaScriptIn-browser to-do list with LocalStorageA weather app that consumes an API with loading and error statesInteractive dashboard with graphs and instant search
HTMLSimple personal pageA complete landing page with a contact formAccessible multi-page template
CSSProfile card with simple effectsResponsive card grid without a frameworkDesign system with light/dark theme variants
C++Guess the number game in the terminalStudent management system with files and STLA small game engine or process simulator built with CMake
Single project rule:

Finishing one complete project (even if simple) is better than five incomplete projects. The complete project introduces you to README, Git, testing, and deployment — and this is what counts at work.

Habits of a professional programmer

The difference between a beginner and a professional is not the number of languages but the way they work. These habits save hours and make your code readable to anyone who works with you.

Read the whole error

The last line of an error message often names the file, line, and reason. Read it before searching.

Break down the problem

If the code does not work, comment out parts and test one at a time. The solution appears faster.

Write clear names

userCount is better than x. A good name needs no comment.

Test edge cases

Try null, error, and maximum values. Most bugs live in edge cases.

Use Git regularly

Make commits small: commit whenever a piece works. Going back is easy.

Read someone else's code

Open-source projects teach patterns and organization you will not find in lessons.

Weekly exercise:

Set aside an hour a week to reread and clean up your old code. You will see your progress and catch bad habits before they become permanent.

Section Four · From idea to published website

How websites are built

A website is not a single page; it is a front end that the user can see, a server that executes the logic, a database that stores the state, a domain and HTTPS certificate, and is published on a host. This section explains each layer individually, and then assembles them into a single request journey from the first click of a button to the displayed page.

In simple wordsEvery website = an interface you see + a server running the logic + a database storing state. The browser asks, the server answers, the database stores.
9 stages Interface + server + database Practical code examples Level: Beginner → Intermediate

1. How does the web work?

The web is built on a simple idea: the client requests and the server responds. The browser is the client, and the server is a device running a program that receives requests and returns pages or data.

Browser→DNS→HTTP Request→Server→Database→HTTP Response

DNS

Converts the site name (example.com) to the server's IP address. Without it, the browser does not know where to go.

HTTP/HTTPS

Request and response protocol. HTTPS is a version encrypted with TLS that prevents eavesdropping and modification.

Browser

It reads HTML, builds the DOM, applies CSS, executes JavaScript, and then draws the page.

When you type a link and press Enter, the following happens:

  1. The browser asks the DNS for the domain's IP address.
  2. The browser opens a TCP connection (then TLS if HTTPS) with the server.
  3. It sends an HTTP request containing the path, headers, and possibly data.
  4. The server executes the required code and asks the database if needed.
  5. Returns an HTTP response in HTML or JSON with a status code (200, 404, 500...).
  6. The browser displays the page, executes scripts, and loads images and files.
Frontend ≠ Backend:

Front end runs on the user's device within the browser. Backend runs on the server and you don't see it — all you see is its output.

2. Front-end: HTML, CSS, and JavaScript

The front end is everything the user sees and interacts with. HTML builds the structure, CSS sets the look, and JavaScript adds behavior and interactivity.

TechnologyRoleExample
HTMLPage structure and its semantic elements.<header> <main> <button>
CSSColors, spacing, layout, and responsiveness.display: grid; color: …
JavaScriptLogic, interaction, and network requests.addEventListener("click", …)

The first complete page — copy it into the file index.html and open it in the browser:

html
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="ar" dir="rtl">
<head>
  <meta charset="UTF-8">
  <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1">
  <title>First page</title>
  <style>
    body { font-family: system-ui; background: #0f172a; color: #e2e8f0; }
    .card { max-width: 420px; margin: 60px auto; padding: 24px; border-radius: 14px; background: #1e293b; }
    button { padding: 10px 16px; border: 0; border-radius: 8px; background: #7c8cf8; cursor: pointer; }
  </style>
</head>
<body>
  <div class="card">
    <h1>Hello 👋</h1>
    <p id="en-msg">Click the button to change the text.</p>
    <button onclick="document.getElementById('msg').textContent = 'Changed successfully ✓'">Try it</button>
  </div>
</body>
</html>
Important rule:

Start with clean, semantic HTML, then CSS, then JavaScript. Most problems come from trying to build everything in JS before the structure is correct.

3. Backend: Server and API interface

The server receives requests, verifies permissions, executes business logic, and interacts with the database. The interface between it and the browser is often API, returning JSON, and the browser is the one that displays it.

javascript
// server.js — A simple server with Node.js and Express
const express = require("express");
const app = express();

app.use(express.json());

app.get("/api/hello", (req, res) => {
  res.json({ message: "Hello from the server 👋" });
});

app.post("/api/users", (req, res) => {
  const { name } = req.body;
  if (!name) return res.status(400).json({ error: "Name required" });
  res.status(201).json({ id: 1, name });
});

app.listen(3000, () => console.log("http://localhost:3000"));

The same idea exists in all languages. Choose what suits your project:

LanguageCommon frameworksSuitable for
JavaScript / TypeScriptExpress · NestJS · Next.jsFull web applications and code sharing between front end and back end.
PythonDjango · Flask · FastAPIRapid development, data, and artificial intelligence.
PHPLaravelWebsites, online stores, and CMS platforms.

4. Database: The place of real data

If the site records users, requests, or articles, the natural place for the data is a database, not text files. A database organizes the data and allows secure searching, linking, and updating.

sql
CREATE TABLE users (
  id SERIAL PRIMARY KEY,
  name TEXT NOT NULL,
  email TEXT UNIQUE NOT NULL,
  created_at TIMESTAMP DEFAULT NOW()
);

INSERT INTO users (name, email)
VALUES ('Ahmed', 'ahmed@example.com');

SELECT id, name, email FROM users
WHERE email = 'ahmed@example.com';

SQL (relational)

PostgreSQL · MySQL — tables, relationships, and JOIN. Suitable for structured data and financial transactions.

NoSQL

MongoDB · Redis — documents or keys/values. Flexible for scaling, irregular data, and caching.

Mandatory security:

String concatenation is prohibited. Use Prepared Statements or ORM to prevent SQL Injection, and validate each input before saving.

5. The request journey: from click to display

Here is a login example: the user enters an email and password and clicks “Log in.” This is what actually happens:

Form→POST /login→Verify→Database→Session/Cookie→Control Panel
1

The interface collects data

JavaScript checks the format (valid email, fields not empty) and then sends a POST request to /api/login.

2

The server checks

It verifies the validity of the input, searches for the user, and compares the password to the saved hash — not to the original text.

3

The server creates a session

The server returns a secure cookie (HttpOnly + Secure + SameSite) or a token, and saves the login state.

4

The browser stores the session

The browser automatically saves cookies and sends them with subsequent requests, and the interface displays the control panel.

5

Every subsequent request is protected

The server verifies the session and permissions before any operation — the interface alone is not where verification happens.

6. Publishing: From your device to the Internet

Once the site is up and running locally, you need a permanent location that is available 24/7. Deployment means transferring files or containers to a host, linking a domain to it, and activating HTTPS.

Hosting typeExamplesBest suited for
Static hostingGitHub Pages · Netlify · VercelHTML/CSS/JS websites and front-end projects.
Application platformsRender · Railway · Fly.ioServer + database without server management.
VPS serverDigitalOcean · Hetzner · AWS EC2Complete control, suitable for learning and understanding the structure.

Basic DNS records

RecordPurpose
AThe domain connects to an IPv4 address.
AAAAThe domain connects to an IPv6 address.
CNAMEMakes a domain point to another domain (such as www).
MXSpecifies the email server.
TXTCheck ownership and SPF/DKIM records.
bash
# 1) Build the project
npm run build

# 2) Upload the files (e.g. rsync to a server you own)
rsync -avz dist/ user@server:/var/www/my-site/

# 3) Free HTTPS via Let's Encrypt
sudo apt install certbot python3-certbot-nginx
sudo certbot --nginx -d example.com -d www.example.com
Test before launch:

Open the site on mobile, test the links, verify the certificate (HTTPS lock), and check the speed. Deployment is not the end — monitoring and backup are part of the job.

7. Site security: the minimum before publishing

  • Always HTTPS: Valid certificate and redirection from HTTP to HTTPS.
  • Validate input on the server: Interface verification is for convenience only, not for security.
  • Hash for passwords: bcrypt or Argon2 with Salt — MD5/SHA1 forbidden.
  • Parameterized queries: Prepared Statements to prevent SQL Injection.
  • Output escaping: Do not put user input into HTML without Escape to prevent XSS.
  • Least privilege: The database and server accounts get only the permissions they need.
  • Backup: Detached from the server, tested on recovery.
javascript
// Password hashing (Node.js)
const bcrypt = require("bcrypt");
const hash = await bcrypt.hash(password, 12);
const ok = await bcrypt.compare(password, hash);

// Essential security headers (Express)
app.use((req, res, next) => {
  res.setHeader("X-Content-Type-Options", "nosniff");
  res.setHeader("X-Frame-Options", "DENY");
  res.setHeader("Referrer-Policy", "strict-origin-when-cross-origin");
  next();
});
It is forbidden to publish secrets:

Do not save database passwords or API keys inside your code or Git repository. Use environment variables, and never commit the .env file.

8. Plan to build a website in 30 days

1

Days 1–5: Idea and structure

Determine the purpose and audience, draw the pages on paper, and write a list of components. Don't start with code before you know what you're building.

2

Days 6–12: Front end

Semantic HTML, then responsive CSS (Mobile First), then JavaScript for interactivity. Try on different screens.

3

Days 13–22: Server and data

Build a simple API, design the tables, add authentication and permissions, and test each path with Postman or curl.

4

Days 23–30: Deployment and improvement

Domain + HTTPS + hosting, then speed, security, and backup, and finally a review of the user experience.

VS Code

Lightweight editor with built-in HTML/CSS/JS and Git plugins.

Git + GitHub

Save each step, and link the automatic deployment to the repository.

DevTools

Check elements, network and errors within the browser.

9. Common errors that prevent the site from working

ProblemMost common causeSolution
The page is whiteJavaScript error stops execution.Open the console and read the first error.
CSS/JS files are not loadingBad path or 404 status.Check the Network tab and verify relative paths.
The site works locally and fails after deploymentEnvironment variables or absolute paths.Set environment variables and use valid paths.
CORS errorThe interface and the server are on two different origins.Allow only valid origins on the server.
User data leakTrust in interface inputs.Validate on the server and use parameterized queries.
Conclusion:

A good website = clear interface + secure server + structured data + reliable deployment. Start small, deploy early, and improve gradually.

10. Pre-deployment checklist

Before you send the link to anyone, go through this list item by item. Most of the problems that appear after publishing could have disappeared within five minutes of review.

ItemHow to verifyPriority
HTTPS works on all pagesOpen the domain and test the http → https redirect.Critical
There are no errors on the consoleDevTools → Console, and reload each page.Critical
All links workCheck internal links and 404s, and try the full list.Critical
The site works on the phoneTry 320px, 390px, and 768px widths.Critical
Images are compressed in a modern formatWebP/AVIF, at a reasonable maximum width.High
Title and description for each page<title> and meta description are different.High
Environment variables on the serverNo keys inside the code or repository.Critical
Backup is enabledRegular backups of the database and files.High
Custom 404 pageTry a path that doesn't exist.Medium
Fonts and icons are loadedNetwork → Check for any failed request.Medium
Basic accessibilityImage alt text, sufficient contrast, keyboard navigation.High
Analytics and error toolsAnalytics + Error Tracking (Sentry or alternative).Medium
Quick test:

Open the website in an incognito window on another device and network. This will reveal problems with cache, sessions, and absolute paths that only work on your device.

11. Performance and SEO: The basics that make the difference

A fast website is not a luxury: every second of delay reduces the number of visitors who convert. Basic SEO is not complicated — a clear title, honest description, and organized content make more difference than any trick.

LCP — largest element

The time until the largest visual element appears. Aim for less than 2.5s: Compress images, prevent broken fonts from loading.

CLS — layout stability

Sudden jumps. Determine the dimensions of your photos and videos in advance, and reserve space for ads.

INP — responsiveness

The speed at which the page reacts to clicks. Reduce heavy JavaScript and break down long tasks.

html
<head>
  <title>Page name — short description</title>
  <meta name="description" content="Clear description in 150 characters">
  <link rel="canonical" href="https://example.com/page">

  <meta property="og:title" content="Page Name">
  <meta property="og:description" content="Description shown when sharing">
  <meta property="og:image" content="https://example.com/cover.jpg">
</head>

<img src="photo.webp" alt="Photo description" width="800" height="600" loading="lazy" >
OptimizationImpact
Image compression and use of WebP/AVIFThe biggest gain is often in speed.
Local fonts with font-display: swapText appears instantly instead of a blank screen.
Load JavaScript when neededFaster page and better interaction.
Logical h1/h2 headingsBetter understanding of content and search engines.
sitemap.xml sitemapFaster indexing of new pages.
Structured data (Schema.org)Richer search results.

Section Five · Save and share your work

Git and GitHub

Git is a version management system that saves the step-by-step history of your project, and GitHub is the platform that hosts that history online for collaboration and sharing. In this section you will learn about the daily cycle, branches, connecting to a remote repository, and Pull Requests — with practical commands starting from the beginning of git init.

In simple wordsGit records snapshots of your project (commits) so you can return to any version. GitHub keeps those snapshots online and enables collaboration.
11 topics Practical commands Realistic scenarios Level: Beginner
Git ≠ GitHub:

Git A local program that runs on your device and saves copies of the project. GitHub A cloud service that hosts Git repositories and adds collaboration, review, and task management. You can use Git without GitHub, but GitHub without Git is meaningless.

1. Basic idea: copies and snapshots

Instead of saving files named project-final-v2 and project-final-ok, Git saves a commit for each change along with a date, message, and author. You can go back to any snapshot, compare it, and find out who changed what and why.

Working Directory→git add→Staging Area→git commit→Local Repo→git push→GitHub

Local first

All commits are saved on your device. The Internet is not a requirement to use Git.

Every commit is a snapshot

It is not just a diff; Git can rebuild the project state at any commit.

Branches are cheap

You can create a branch to test an idea and isolate it from the main branch without fear.

2. First-time installation and setup

Install Git and set your name and email. The email here is the one that will appear in every commit, and it is preferable that it be the same as the GitHub email so that commits are linked correctly.

bash
sudo apt install git -y
git --version

git config --global user.name "your-name"
git config --global user.email "you@example.com"
git config --global init.defaultBranch main

# Review the settings
git config --list
Note:

If you use more than one device, use the same name and email. You can omit --global if you want different settings for just one project.

3. The daily cycle

These are the commands that you will repeat every day: edit files, add them to the staging area, and then commit. Try it on your first demo project.

bash
mkdir my-project && cd my-project
git init

echo "# My Project" > README.md
git status
git add README.md
git commit -m "Add a README file"

git log --oneline
git diff
AreaMeaningCommand
Working DirectoryThe files you are currently editing.git status
Staging AreaThe changes you staged for commit.git add <file>
Local RepositoryHistory of commits saved in .git.git commit -m "..."

4. Essential daily commands

CommandPurpose
git statusShows modified files and what is staged.
git add .Adds all changes in the current folder to the staging area.
git commit -m "message"Saves a snapshot of the staged changes.
git log --oneline --graphVisually concise history of branches and merges.
git diffThe difference between the current files and the last commit.
git restore <file>Discards unsaved changes to a file.
git restore --staged <file>Unstages the file without discarding its changes.

5. Branches and merges

Branch: A separate version of the project history. Use a branch for each feature or fix, and return to the main branch when you're done. This prevents you from breaking main during the experiment.

bash
git branch
git switch -c feature/login

# Edit the files, then:
git add .
git commit -m "Add a login page"

git switch main
git merge feature/login
git branch -d feature/login
If a conflict occurs:

Git stops the merge and flags conflicting lines in the file. Open the file, select the correct version or merge them manually, delete the conflict markers, then git add and git commit.

6. Link to GitHub

Create a new repository on GitHub without a README or .gitignore (so it doesn't conflict with your local project), then link it and upload the first commit.

bash
git remote add origin https://github.com/USERNAME/REPO.git
git branch -M main
git push -u origin main

# After the first time
git push

# Clone an existing repository
git clone https://github.com/USERNAME/REPO.git

# Pull the latest updates
git pull origin main

Replace USERNAME and REPO with your account name and repository name. The command git push -u connects the local branch to the remote branch, then the word git push is sufficient.

7. Set up SSH (optional but recommended)

Instead of entering the username and token every time, use an SSH key. Generate the key, add the public key in GitHub under Settings → SSH and GPG keys, and then test the connection.

bash
ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C "you@example.com"
eval "$(ssh-agent -s)"
ssh-add ~/.ssh/id_ed25519

# Copy the public key and add it on GitHub
cat ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub

# Test the connection
ssh -T git@github.com

After setup use the SSH link to the repository: git@github.com:USERNAME/REPO.git. Never share the private key id_ed25519 — only the public key is shared.

8. Pull Request and Collaboration

In teams, never push directly to main. Make a branch, push it, and open a Pull Request to review the code before merging.

bash
git switch -c feature/new-section
git add .
git commit -m "Add a new section"
git push -u origin feature/new-section

# After merging the PR on GitHub
git switch main
git pull origin main
git branch -d feature/new-section
1

Open Pull Request

From the repository page on GitHub, click Compare & pull request, and write a clear description of the change.

2

See the code

Request a review from a colleague, and respond to comments with new edits to the same branch.

3

Merge and delete the branch

After approval click Merge, then delete the remote branch and update main locally.

9. The .gitignore file

A single file in the project root prevents Git from tracking temporary and sensitive files. Make this file from the first day before any commit.

gitignore
node_modules/
.venv/
__pycache__/
.env
*.log
.DS_Store

If a file was accidentally added before you added it to .gitignore, use git rm -r --cached node_modules to remove it from tracking while keeping it on your device.

10. Undo errors

StatusCommandNote
Undo unsaved modificationsgit restore <file>It cannot be recovered after execution.
Unstage a filegit restore --staged <file>Your changes are preserved.
Edit the last commitgit commit --amend -m "..."Only before pushing.
Undo the last commit but keep the changesgit reset --soft HEAD~1Relatively safe.
Undo the commit and discard its changesgit reset --hard HEAD~1Dangerous — permanently discards changes.
Undo a commit on a shared branchgit revert <hash>Creates a new commit that reverses the change — better for teams.
Temporarily save changesgit stash / git stash popTo quickly switch between tasks.

11. Best practices

  • Make commits small and clear: one commit per idea, and a message that begins with a verb: add, fix, refactor.
  • Pull before you push: git pull --rebase reduces unnecessary merge commits.
  • Don't keep secrets: .env files, keys and tokens are always preferred outside of Git.
  • Review the diff before committing: git diff --staged shows exactly what will be saved.
  • Branch for each task: Even if you work alone, the branches organize the project history.
  • Don't rewrite history on a shared branch: Use revert instead of reset --hard.

12. Solve common problems

Most Git messages are clear if you know how to read them. These are the most famous messages you will encounter, their meaning, and the direct solution.

Message / problemCauseSolution
not a git repositoryYou are out of the project folder.cd into the project or run git init.
nothing to commitThere are no changes in Staging.git status then git add.
rejected (fetch first)The remote branch has the most recent commits.git pull --rebase, then git push.
Permission denied (publickey)The SSH key is not added to your account.Add the key in GitHub or use HTTPS + Token.
fatal: refusing to merge unrelated historiesTwo repositories with unrelated histories.git pull --allow-unrelated-histories.
Conflict in a fileThe same line changed in two places.Edit the file manually, remove the conflict markers, then git add and commit.
commit on the wrong branchYou forgot to run git checkout.git branch fix, then git reset --hard HEAD~1 on the wrong branch.
I accidentally deleted a commitA hard reset (reset --hard).git reflog then git reset --hard <hash>.
I accidentally committed a secret fileAn .env file or a key committed by mistake.Immediately revoke the key, then git rm --cached and add the file to .gitignore.
The repository became very heavyLarge files or videos in history.Use Git LFS or git filter-repo.
bash
# Restore a file to its state before the last commit
git restore path/to/file

# See every branch movement (even deleted ones)
git reflog

# Return to a specific commit from the reflog
git reset --hard HEAD@{2}

# Clean untracked files (careful: permanent deletion)
git clean -nd   # preview first
git clean -fd
Before any command with --hard or clean -fd:

Commit or git stash first. These commands delete your edits without the Recycle Bin, and the reflog only saves commits and not untracked files.
